‘Zi’ Review: Kogonada Rediscovers His Voice with a Microbudget Hong Kong Experiment Starring Michelle Mao and Haley Lu Richardson

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Editor’s Note: This review was originally published during the 2026 Sundance Film Festival. Grasshopper Film releases “Zi” in select theaters starting Friday, November 6.

A Hong Kong violinist who’s losing her grip on reality, young Zi (Michelle Mao) is haunted by visions of her future self. The doctors at the local neurology center suspect that she might be suffering from the early symptoms of a brain tumor, but Zi — a being of pure cinema, cobbled together from the half-formed memories of films like “La Jetée” and “Cléo from 5 to 7” — will have to wait a long and lonely 24 hours for the test results to come back.  Related Stories ‘To Hold a Mountain’ Took on the Government — Now It’s Montenegro’s Oscar Submission ‘Soul Patrol’ Review: The Vietnam War’s First Black Special Ops Team Shares Their Stories in An Enlightening Documentary

Well, maybe not so lonely. Crying on one of the innumerable concrete steps that climb Lantau Island, Zi is consoled by a sweet American expat with a strangely familiar face (and an aggressively unnatural blonde wig). Her name is L, she’s a disillusioned former dancer played by the luminous Haley Lu Richardson, and she can’t help but reciprocate the inexplicable closeness that Zi feels towards her. Over the course of a single night together, these two lost souls will help to relocate each other in time — to resynchronize with the world around them, reconnect with the infinite potential of the present, and return themselves to the creative purpose that once anchored them to it.

Even if seen without a shred of context, it would be obvious to anyone that “Zi” was made by someone who is — or hopefully was — desperate to spark a realignment of his own. To steal himself away from regret and anxiety and tether his perspective back to the now. Shot without a script or a budget in the immediate aftermath of Kogonada’s ill-received “A Big Bold Beautiful Journey” (the “After Yang” auteur’s studio debut, by all indications a soul-crushing experience for an artist whose two previous features retained the delicate grace of the video essays that he used to make), this searching and unformed wisp of an experiment hardly represents the first time that an ascendent director has scaled down after a misstep, but “Zi” takes the notion of “one for me” to much greater and more galvanizing extremes than usual.

This isn’t just a filmmaker trying to get back on track, it’s a filmmaker desperately, nakedly searching for a way to fall back in love with filmmaking itself. A way to prove that it might still love him back. (Kogonada described the project to IndieWire as “A retreat, as well as a pursuit.”)

While the prospect of watching Kogonada do that will naturally be of greater interest to those who have watched him get to this point, this movie’s palpably improvised nature — its stolen shots and recursive shape lend the impression of someone trying to cut their ultra-evocative vacation footage into a coherent narrative — would force anyone to wonder about the meta-textual aspects of its creation. “You feel like your sole purpose in life is something, and it doesn’t choose you back,” L sputters out at one point. “What do you do with that? I had to get away from it all. That’s why I came here. Wandering.”

Since arriving in Hong Kong, she’s made a habit of collecting sounds in much the same way as Kogonada has come there on the hunt for images. They both find a dissonance around Victoria Harbor that reflects their internal feelings of otherness and self-alienation. It’s China, but not. It’s a single city, but one split across two main areas by a stretch of the sea that some people never cross. It’s among the world’s most densely populated metropolises, but the vertical terrain seems fundamentally inhospitable to human civilization, and nature continues to assert itself between every skyscraper, shopping mansion, and crack in the sidewalk. Zi is the only native character in the film, but the history and design of Hong Kong reflects her disassociation as well — like her, the former British colony’s experience of the present is delayed.

That’s the closest thing to a diagnosis we get for what ails her, though it’s clear that Kogonada is leaning more towards soft science-fiction than the stuff of hard medicine. “Temoral relativism” is how L’s ex-fiancé Min (Jin Ha) describes it, though he’s slow to reveal how much he knows about Zi’s condition. Everything she perceives is already in the past. I suppose that’s true for all of us to some degree, if you factor in the nanoseconds that elapse between stimuli and our ability to perceive it, but the situation is more pronounced for Zi (who Mao plays with the effervescent helplessness of a paper airplane). Of course, that doesn’t explain why she believes she’s bearing witness to visions of her future self, but that confusion only exacerbates her feelings of distance from the present.

Even in its more platonic dimensions, the threadbare story that gradually allows Zi to close that gap is awash with the romance of loss, reconciliation, and belonging. Kogonada is falling back in love with the movies right before our eyes, and yet,  in characteristic fashion, that process isn’t garish or forced. His is a vision that is revealed through careful subtraction — by clearing away all of the dust and smudges that came to obscure it.

While Kogonada indulges in a few legible winks to his cinematic forebears (the most obvious of those being a step-printed homage to Wong Kar Wai, and my most far-fetched being a disoriented nod to José Luis Guerín’s “In the City of Sylvia”), he and cinematographer Benjamin Loeb almost completely eschew the neon exoticism that foreigners tend to look for in Hong Kong, with the vast majority of the film being captured in unfussy handheld shots that accentuate its here-and-now anxiety and delegate the emotional heavy lifting to an impeccably curated soundtrack of Ryuichi Sakamoto remixes and originals.

By the end, “Zi” engenders a certain emotional attachment toward and between its characters, all of it building to a gently unexpected gutpunch of a final minute. But even that last-minute reveal — not a plot twist so much as a sudden change in perspective — is rooted more in the movie’s form than its plot, as Kogonada’s characters remain largely symbolic of their function in the writer-director’s journey back to himself.

It’s a testament to Mao, Ha, and especially Richardson — who continues to be so good at grounding Kogonada’s most abstract ideas in a tender human place — that “Zi” retains even a soft dramatic pull as it searches for the present, though its recursive nature certainly wasn’t conceived with a casual audience in mind. If you’re hooked, which I wasn’t, or haunted by it, which I was, that will likely have less to do with an acute emotional connection to these characters than with the overflowing rewards of watching someone rediscover the sound of their own voice, and hear a way forward into the future in its echoes.

“Zi” premiered at the 2026 Sundance Film Festival. Grasshopper Film releases it in select theaters starting Friday, November 6.

The Yangwang U9 Xtreme Just Set Two Guinness World Speed Records

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The electric Chinese supercar went more than 312 mph on a public road in the Abu Dhabi desert.

The fastest production car in the world. No, not necessarily an attribute that would be useful in your real life, unless your driveway is a section of unrestricted autobahn, but as an achievement, it's something automakers love to boast about. Porsche. Ferrari. Bugatti. And now Yangwang.

The luxury division of Chinese giant BYD (as Lexus is to Toyota), Yangwang's appropriately named U9 Xtreme hypercar just hit 312 mph on a public road in Abu Dhabi. This feat nets it two Guinness World Records at once: the single-direction highest speed for both a production car and for a production electric car.

At this point, perhaps the electric production speed record doesn't need its own subdivision, especially since the U9 was chiefly breaking its own previous record of 308 mph, set last year. The U9 Xtreme, or U9X, has four electric motors, one at each wheel, for a grand total of over 3000 hp. That's three Veyrons concentrated into a single car.

See all results for used cars for sale near 99504

The battery architecture is also distinct from the standard U9, with a 1200-volt architecture and the ability to both send and receive huge amounts of power with greatly improved thermal efficiency. Even when nearly depleted, at 20 percent charge, it can still pump out 1800 kilowatts of power (2414 hp), or soak up 700 kilowatts through regenerative braking.

A motor at each corner means almost limitless fine-tuning for torque-vectoring control, meting out all that power with minimal wheelslip. The U9X also features electric dampers that clamp down on squat and dive under hard acceleration and braking, and active aerodynamic elements to maintain stability at speed. A tip of the hat also goes to Indonesia's Giti Tires, who developed the rubber capable of putting up with these tremendous speeds and forces.

Yangwang only plans to build thirty of these U9X models, and it's not like you can just slap on a license plate and bully 911 Turbos on the Autobahn. The 4.35-mile stretch of public road used for the record attempt was closed at the time, and also fastidiously cleaned of debris, including going back over the surface looking for any wires left by the street sweepers.

However, as a demonstration of just how advanced BYD's battery chemistry can be, this new speed record represents all kinds of bragging rights. Speed records come and go. For now, nothing can touch the Yangwang U9 Xtreme.

Treasury cuts off MEXC Global over alleged $16 billion in transactions linked to Iran, North Korea and Russia

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

On Friday, the Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network issued an order against MEXC Global and said it was a money laundering concern.

The U.S. Treasury Department says it has severed crypto exchange MEXC Global from the United States after it allegedly supported $16 billion in transactions connected to Iran, North Korea, and Russia.

On Friday, the Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network issued an order against MEXC Global and said it was a money laundering concern.

"Financial institutions cannot have it both ways: Either you support the Iranian regime, or you have access to the U.S. financial system," said Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent in a statement. "Treasury will continue to sever the Iranian regime’s economic lifelines and thwart its desperate attempts to circumvent U.S. sanctions using digital assets."

Over the past year, Treasury has implemented a crackdown of sorts on crypto platforms allegedly helping Iran evade sanctions amid its ongoing war against the country. In June, Iran's largest crypto exchange, Nobitex, was sanctioned after Treasury said it processed more than half of Iran's crypto inflows in 2025. In September, the U.S. also sanctioned Iranian crypto exchange BitBank over alleged bitcoin transfers to the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps.

On Friday, Treasury said that though MEXC Global said it maintained anti-money laundering and says it will block the use of people using it in countries including Iran and North Korea, that it had "severe deficiencies" in those AML controls. The department said Friday's sanction will protect the U.S. financial system.

"Today's action will protect the U.S. financial system from the significant illicit finance threats posed by MEXC Global," the department said.

Report: Surprise €10m deal tabled for Liverpool veteran

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Liverpool have a lot of decisions that they need to make on their squad over the next few months.

A handful of their first-team squad all have their contracts expiring in June 2027 and, with no negotiations lined up for any of them, another massive overhaul could be just around the corner.

Of course, the number of transfers that Liverpool have been linked with is obscene but, at least it proves that the Reds are casting their net far and wide for their next transfers.

Some positions are obviously more in need of reinforcement than others but, since Liverpool could end up moving some of their own players on as well, nobody actually knows what the next year or so could look like for the Anfield outfit.

That is, especially when multi-million deals are put up for grabs.

AC Milan want to offer Virgil van Dijk €10 million per year to join them from Liverpool

Virgil van Dijk has easily been one of Liverpool's best players ever since he joined.

No matter who he's been partnered with or who the Reds have faced, the Dutchman has always managed to come out as one of the stand-out performers.

Indeed, things have gone a bit wrong for him every now and then but, more often than not, he's proven why he's one of the world's best centre-backs.

However, now that he's 35 years old, questions are being asked about how long he can keep these performance levels up for.

Liverpool have already started to line up players such as Giovanni Leoni and Jeremy Jacquet to replace him but, right now, Andoni Iraola's men would be lost without their captain in the starting XI.

So, that's why the latest update from MilanNews24 is so surprising.

"According to what MilanNews24 has learned, Cardinale would like to make a concrete attempt," they wrote.

"It's not just a tempting idea or a name linked to AC Milan to fuel the fans' ambitions: the Rossoneri owner is reportedly willing to seriously evaluate the conditions for bringing the Dutchman to Italy.

"To land Van Dijk, there are two key conditions. The first concerns Champions League qualification , a crucial element in presenting the defender with an ambitious and competitive sporting project.

"The second is naturally economic: a two-year contract worth 8-10 million euros per season would be on the table .

"A significant figure, which testifies to the scope of the investment envisioned by Cardinale.

"AC Milan is therefore dreaming of an international signing for the summer of 2027. Van Dijk would be a signing capable of bringing experience, leadership, and personality. Now they need to turn that dream into a concrete possibility."

Virgil van Dijk is still one of Liverpool's most important players

As mentioned, Van Dijk is key to the way that Liverpool play at the moment.

When he's left on the bench for a cup tie or a less important game, the difference in quality is staggering.

The Dutchman may not seem like he does all that much when he's on the pitch but, in reality, he's the glue that holds the team together.

Of course, other players such as Ronald Araujo or Joe Gomez can try and plug the gap left by the Dutchman but, neither of them are anywhere near his level.

It was always going to be a worry that Van Dijk would leave for free at the end of this season but, the fact that a concrete bid is about to come from AC Milan is surprising.

Most reports that emerge from Italy make it clear that most Serie A clubs are skint with several outfits often linked with haggling Liverpool for a discount on Federico Chiesa's impending transfer.

So, the fact that AC Milan are willing to give Van Dijk around €10 million per year is shocking.

His stature and name in the game will obviously carry a lot of weight but, unless you're a team who knows you can afford his wages such as Liverpool, it feels a tad strange for him to be targeted by anyone else.

Investors brace for lower Anthropic valuation, calling $1.5 trillion a 'stretch'

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Following tech's selloff on Wall Street this week, some investors are lowering their expectations for Anthropic.

The artificial intelligence company had been looking toward a $2 trillion valuation in its IPO up until recently, but stakeholders told CNBC that even $1.5 trillion is now in doubt, with one investor calling that number a "stretch."

The change in sentiment comes alongside growing concerns about the outsized spending by AI companies and a sharp post-midterm slump in the market. The Nasdaq plummeted more than 5% on Thursday, its steepest drop in 19 months, reflecting fears that Democrats' newfound power in Washington, D.C., and a nationwide rebuke of AI will lead to a slowdown in the buildout of data centers.

Bearishness on the AI trade was on the rise before the election. The Nasdaq suffered its worst month in October since March 2025, led by declines in chipmakers and neoclouds.

Anthropic is set to list on the Nasdaq, under the ticker ANT, and is gearing up for an expected debut in a matter of weeks. Last month, Anthropic was reportedly seeking a valuation as high as $2 trillion. As of May, the company was privately valued at $965 billion.

With the latest market turmoil, one investor said $1 trillion to $1.25 trillion is a more likely valuation target, although that could change with market conditions. The investors asked not to be named because they're not authorized to speak about the matter.

Gil Luria, an analyst at D.A. Davidson, told CNBC that, "Investors will expect the IPO to be priced at least at the low end of previous expectations."

"The market really needs this IPO to go well and the bankers on the deal surely understand that," Luria said. "The best way to set Anthropic up for success is to price the deal well."

The company is expected to kick off its roadshow next week.

What's that bright 'star' in the early morning sky? It's not a star at all!

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Spotted a dazzling light in the eastern sky before sunrise? It's Venus, and the planet is only going to get brighter as it heads toward its spectacular peak later this month.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Early morning risers heading to work or school before sunrise are likely to notice a very bright, star-like object appearing low in the east-southeast sky just before sunrise. What is somewhat amazing is that just a couple of weeks ago this object wasn't there at all, but now it literally stands out with such brilliance as to almost command the attention of commuters who might be traveling in an easterly direction to "Look at me!"

The object in question is, of course, the planet Venus, which is currently amid one of its most spectacular forays into the morning sky, literally "vaulting up" into prominence.

On Oct. 24, Venus came to inferior conjunction, passing between the Earth and sun and shifting from the evening into the morning sky. And the view is only going to get better in the days and weeks to come!

In terms of morning visibility, Venus is now in a literal and figurative sense "rising to the occasion." It's now rising before the break of dawn, more than 90 minutes before sunrise. From here on, it will rise three minutes earlier each day, so that by Dec. 17 it will rise 3 hours and 50 minutes before sunrise and blaze in a completely dark sky for more than two hours before the first vestige of morning twilight.

It will reach peak brilliance on Nov. 29, when it will shine at an eye-popping magnitude of -4.9. That's 12 times brighter than its nearest planetary competitor, Jupiter and a full 25 times more dazzling than Sirius, the brightest of all stars. If you live in a rural location with no light pollution to interfere, you might be able to make out that it is casting a visible shadow and if you follow it right on through sunrise, it will be evident to your naked eye in full daylight: a tiny white speck plainly evident against the blue daytime sky.

In a way, we could even regard Venus as this year's version of a "Christmas Star," glowing with stunning radiance ". . . in the east" like the biblical quote mentioned in St. Matthew.

After reaching its high point in terms of morning visibility in mid-December, Venus will start a long, slow slide back toward the sun, rising about 1½ minutes later each morning from New Year's Day through late March. By then, Venus will be coming up as dawn is breaking, roughly 100 minutes before sunrise. Venus will continue to get gradually lower and rise progressively later during the spring and by late June it will be coming up less than an hour before sunrise, ultimately vanishing into the bright dawn twilight by July. Venus will then go on a "summer sabbatical" of sorts and will not reappear again until about this time next year when it will begin a very fine evening apparition.

So, if any of your friends or relatives remark to you that they saw a "strange UFO" on the way to work or school recently, you now can set them straight.

Joe Rao serves as an instructor and guest lecturer at New York's Hayden Planetarium. He writes about astronomy for Natural History magazine, Sky and Telescope, The Old Farmer's Almanac and other publications.

He writes about astronomy for Natural History magazine, Sky & Telescope and other publications. A farmer in Mexico saw the ground split open in his cornfield, then watched a volcano grow to 1,391 feet tall

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

In 1943, Mexico's Parícutin volcano erupted from a farmer's cornfield, growing rapidly and burying two villages during nine years of activity.

On February 20, 1943, farmer Dionisio Pulido watched a fissure open in his field near the village of Parícutin, in the Mexican state of Michoacán. Smoke rose from the crack, along with a hissing sound and a smell of sulfur.

Within a week, the new cone stood more than 490 feet (150 meters) tall. When the eruption ended in 1952, it had risen 1,391 feet (424 meters) above the cornfield where it began.

Lava buried two villages along the way. Parícutin was the first volcano whose eruption modern scientists observed in detail from its birth, according to the International Union of Geological Sciences (IUGS).

Starting around February 5 to 7, 1943, local witnesses felt about two weeks of earthquake activity before the eruption.

Felipe Cuara Amezcua, then municipal president of San Juan Parangaricutiro, later reported that the tremors grew until more than 200 shook the area in a single day.

On February 20, Pulido went to his farm to prepare the fields for the spring sowing. He had owned the land for 31 years.

Smithsonian mineralogist William F. Foshag and Mexican geologist Jenaro González R. later collected his account and his wife’s. A 1956 U.S. Geological Survey bulletin printed them in English translation.

At about 4 p.m., Pulido left his wife, Paula, and went to burn a pile of branches. She had been hearing underground noise and thunder.

“About 4 p. m., after talking to my husband, I heard a kind of loud whistle,” Paula Pulido recalled.

“I saw that it was a kind of fissure that had only a depth of half a meter,” Pulido said.

He then saw the ground swell by about 6.6 to 8 feet (2 to 2.5 meters), while gray smoke or dust rose from the crack.

“Immediately more smoke began to rise, with a hiss or whistle, loud and continuous,” he recounted.

Frightened, he mounted his mare and galloped to the village where his family was waiting.

Accounts from Pulido and other witnesses gave “the first adequate account of the birth of a new volcano,” Foshag and González wrote.

One of the first photographs of the eruption, taken at 6 p.m. on February 20, shows an ash plume rising from the new vent. By midnight, the cone was about 20 feet (6 meters) high.

On the morning of February 21, a strong earthquake caused panic. People from Parícutin fled to San Juan Parangaricutiro, and many from San Juan went on to Angahuan or Uruapan.

A photograph from February 21 shows the cone about 100 feet (30 meters) tall, just rising above the treetops. After six days of activity, it reached 548 feet (167 meters).

The first lava flow, called the Quitzocho flow, moved north over cornfields prepared for planting. Mexican geologist Ezequiel Ordóñez estimated that the early lava advanced about 10 feet (3 meters) an hour.

Ash fell too. It piled up deeper than 6 inches (15 centimeters) over an area of 116 square miles (300 square kilometers) around the volcano.

When the first phase of activity ended on October 19, 1943, the original vent had built a cone about 1,200 feet (365 meters) high.

Pulido’s home village lay only 1.9 miles (3 kilometers) northwest of the new cone. Its 733 residents had to abandon it for good four months after the eruption began, and lava later flowed over the site.

Starting in January 1944, a new phase sent out the Taquí lava flows, which spread over about 7 square miles (18 square kilometers) by 1945.

In July 1944, lava surrounded the church of San Juan Parangaricutiro. Its tower still stands in the middle of a lava flow, the IUGS notes.

In all, the volcano’s lavas reached up to 2.5 miles (4 kilometers) from the vent and formed a field of about 9.7 square miles (25 square kilometers).

The Mexican government gave displaced residents new land in Caltzontzín, 17 miles (27 kilometers) to the southeast.

Parícutin was “the first well-documented example of an eruption of a monogenetic volcanic cone in historical time,” according to a 1993 USGS film report.

A monogenetic cone forms during a single eruptive episode. Parícutin, active for about nine years, gave scientists an unprecedented chance to follow such a volcano through its full life cycle.

An international group documented the activity in real time. Celedonio Gutiérrez, a resident of San Juan who kept a diary from the first day, worked with U.S. scientists throughout the eruption.

In 1990, volcanologists I. Yokoyama and S. de la Cruz-Reyna analyzed the precursory earthquakes of the 1943 eruption, a type of signal that comes before eruptions.

In 1985, decades after the eruption stopped, a fumarole at Parícutin still measured 883°F (473°C). The cone is one of more than 1,400 vents in the Michoacán-Guanajuato volcanic field.

The information was obtained from the Smithsonian Institution’s Global Volcanism Program.

‘To Hold a Mountain’ Took on the Government — Now It’s Montenegro’s Oscar Submission

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

For one of the most important screenings of “To Hold a Mountain,” the filmmakers had to bring their own electricity. In July, co-directors Biljana Tutorov and Petar Glomazić hauled a tent, a generator, and a DCP up to the Montenegrin highlands where they had spent years filming.

The creative couple, also partnered in life, wanted the shepherds who lived and worked in Sinjajevina to see the movie they had made possible. But by then, Montenegro had already resolved their story’s central conflict — scrapping the government’s plan to turn these people’s ancestral pastures into a military training ground after joining NATO in 2017. Related Stories ‘Soul Patrol’ Review: The Vietnam War’s First Black Special Ops Team Shares Their Stories in An Enlightening Documentary Could ‘The Further Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth’ Re-Ignite Quentin Tarantino’s Bruce Lee Controversy?

“To Hold a Mountain” won the World Cinema Grand Jury Prize for documentary at Sundance, and Montenegro’s prime minister and president even showed up to the shepherds’ screening with their families, unannounced. When the film opens at New York’s Film Forum on December 16, American audiences will see the feature’s updated conclusion.

“We reopened the film to continue shooting and then a second end happened that was very empowering and powerful,” Tutorov said at IndieWire’s Art of the Doc series.

During our conversation, the filmmaker recalled arriving in Sinjajevina with Glomazić on the first day of protest. They wanted to help the community and make a movie, and agreed early on that those were two different jobs.

“We will separate our activist mission from our filmmaker’s mission,” she said. “That was a very good decision because it allowed us to be very bold in both roles.”

Onscreen, the fight over who would get to lay claim to the mountain centered on two people who already did: Gara, a shepherd and community leader, and Nada, the young girl she was raising. As the threat to their home brought a history of violence in their own family to the surface, the directors had to reckon with how much of Nada’s private life might become public.

“We really wanted it to be an empowering experience for [her],” Tutorov said.

Nada was twelve and a half when the filmmakers began to understand what the story would ask of her, and Tutorov said they decided to keep filming until she turned eighteen — precisely so Nada could decide for herself whether she wanted to be part of it. The crew logged more than 230 shooting days, though some yielded only half an hour of footage.

Gara and Nada had work to do, too. The directors and cinematographer Eva Kraljević shared a small shepherd’s shed, assisting with various chores including cows that had to be milked twice a day. Over successive summers, their routine became a framing device. Tutorov, who studied art history, thinks of the mountain as a kind of chorus bearing witness to living things on it.

Last July, just as the filmmakers were wrapping up, Montenegro’s government canceled the training-ground plan. Tutorov believes “To Hold a Mountain” and how it told the existing community’s story influenced that decision. She also pointed out that a different government was in power by 2025, following years of organizing by Sinjajevina locals.

At the shepherd’s screening a year later, Prime Minister Milojko Spajić said the film reinforced his view that canceling the plan had been the right call. Then Montenegro chose “To Hold a Mountain” as its official submission for the international feature Oscar. Tutorov said there was irony in realizing that, after years of fighting the government, she and Glomazić would be proudly representing the country on a global stage.

“It feels like they really kind of destroyed our strategy,” she said, warmly.

It’s a good joke, and an even better reason to remember that rough-and-tumble showing. For an evening, the people who’d spent years making their case to the government got to host its leaders on the very land they’d fought to keep. Tutorov recalled what Montenegro’s president told the filmmakers after seeing the movie: “Thank you guys for teaching us democracy.”

President Trump Moves to Make Oval Office a Public Monument

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

President Donald Trump signed a proclamation Thursday seeking to designate the Oval Office and the adjacent Cabinet Room national monuments, saying that the new designation would prevent future administrations from making major modifications without Congressional approval. In his second term, Trump made it a priority to redecorate both, turning the previously restrained presidential working spaces into extravagantly gilded interiors in the style of Louis XIV.

In the wake of this week’s midterm elections, in which Republicans suffered historic defeats, Trump appears to be self-soothing by focusing on the many renovation projects he has initiated at the White House and across Washington, D.C. At the White House, in addition to his makeover of the Oval Office, these have included the construction of a granite helipad on the White House’s South Lawn and, most notoriously, the demolition of the building’s East Wing to make way for a 90,000-square-foot, $600 million ballroom. (On Wednesday, in his first public appearance after Tuesday’s Republican wipeout, Trump led a media tour of the ballroom construction site.) Related Articles After a Democratic Victory in Midterm Elections, Trump Focuses on Touting His Ballroom: 'This Is a Special Structure' In Fiery Court Filing, Democratic Lawmaker Says Kennedy Center's Closure Is Meant to Hide Its Failure Under Trump

Around the nation’s capital, Trump has embarked on a fraught, $14 million repair of the Lincoln Memorial Reflecting Pool, begun the redevelopment of a popular public golf course by cutting down hundreds of trees, and proposed a 250-foot-high triumphal arch near Arlington National Cemetery. Apparently worried that incoming Democrats would block construction of the arch, Trump asked for hastened approval of the project, which was granted Wednesday by his hand-picked planning commission; that evening, Judge Tanya Chutkan stopped construction for fourteen days.

It is not clear how designating the Oval Office a national monument will prevent another president from simply revising, or ignoring, the designation—the White House is already designated a National Historic Landmark, which has not stopped Trump from building his ballroom. Nevertheless, speaking from the now gold-saturated Oval Office, Trump told reporters, “When completed, and when we get the documentation fully done, legally binding, it’ll make it impossible for anybody ever to destroy this incredible space.”

Chainalysis says hackers stole credentials and tokens for all of its customers

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Company said a “multifront cyberattack” included AI agents and an on-path data snatch.

Blockchain analysis firm Chainalysis said that hackers have obtained sensitive personal information—including names, usernames, password hashes, and live tokens—for all of its users following a breach of its network security.

The accomplishment could be a major coup for the attackers. Both Chainalysis and public sources have revealed a long list of high-profile customers, including government agencies such as the FBI, US Immigration and Customs Enforcement agency, and the US Air Force, as well as private companies such as Microsoft, Coinbase, and Barclays. It’s unclear if the data theft exposed customers to additional threats.

Last Friday, Chainalysis said it had been the target of a cybersecurity incident in October that was similar to the hacks of other platforms during the same month. The external actor used AI agent-assisted offensive techniques to gain unauthorized access to Chainalysis systems and internal tools. An email the company sent to affected customers said some “non-sensitive customer data, such as user IDs, full names, email addresses, and organization role labels for users provisioned on our platform were accessed.” It’s not clear why the company considered such information “non-sensitive.”

In a Wednesday update, the company said that part of the “ongoing multifront cyberattack” involved an “on-path attack” that exposed a subset of customer passwords, and that the attack vector had been identified and fixed. Also known as an adversary-in-the-middle attack, on-path attacks happen when a hacker gains access to a piece of infrastructure sending traffic back and forth between two parties. Chainalysis has yet to say where the infrastructure compromise occurred and if the data obtained from it was TLS encrypted.

The company said later that day that an investigation two days earlier (on Monday) had revealed that “certain customer information including names, usernames, password hashes and live tokens for all Chainalysis users were exposed to a malicious actor.” It also said it had detected “abnormal activity” and, in response, took down its login page and know-your-transaction service, which monitors threats to cryptocurrency holdings in real time. The company has also removed access to its Reactor and Data Solutions services.

In an update on Friday, Chainalysis said it had begun phasing in customer access to KYT and Address Screening and expected more customers to follow. It asked customers to submit their trusted IP addresses so they can be added to an allow list and to prepare to reset credentials once they regain access. The credential rotation is mandatory.

The company says its investigation has yet to reveal any evidence that any investigation data has been accessed or Chainalysis attribution data has been manipulated.

Several important questions remain unanswered. As noted, the source that made the on-path attack possible is still unknown. It’s not clear how useful the information obtained in such an attack would be unless the attackers also had the ability to decrypt the traffic they were monitoring. If that were the case, the attackers might have gained broad control of the company’s infrastructure.

Chainalysis also didn’t explain why it waited until Wednesday to disclose the data theft of all customers’ personal information when its investigation discovered the exposure two days earlier. Further, the company has provided no details about the AI agents used in the attack and how they worked.

A Chainalysis representative said the company wasn’t commenting beyond the statements in its blog post.

Gradually, the stats could pick up along with the usage of Shibarium, the community’s native Layer-2 chain.

How to Judge Trophy Pronghorn in the Field

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Slipping up to the crest of the sage-flecked knoll, we peered down at a pair of stunning tan-and-white pronghorns. “What a buck,” I said, eyeing the closer one. “He’ll go close to 15 inches.”

“Yep,” my guide replied. “Now, if he’ll move a bit, we’ll get a better look at the real trophy behind him.” Making a classic novice’s mistake, I had assumed the taller buck closest to us was superior.

No sight is more emblematic of Western hunting than a pronghorn buck with jet-black horns staring back at you, poised to prance across the prairie at 70 mph. But just how good of a trophy is he? How thick and impressive will those horns be when you wrap your hands around them? Pronghorns are notoriously difficult to judge in the field. So I spoke to three veteran guides who have a century of analyzing antelope horns among them: Bill Ferranti of New Mexico, Bob Daugherty of Colorado, and Pete Dube of Wyoming.

Most hunters focus on this. A 12-inch antelope is average; 13, good; 14, exceptional. Record-book pronghorns often tape 15 to 16 inches. But horns that jut straight up appear longer than they really are, while those curling back and inward, or with ivory tips, ­appear shorter.

The Solution: Try to view the buck from multiple angles so you can add the curl of the horn tips to your length estimate.

Tip: Use the distance between the tip of the animal’s nose and its eye (about 8 inches) or between its ears (12 inches) as a guide to horn length. Also look for a buck whose prongs protrude from the horn an inch or more above its ears.

These are measured from the tip of the prong, along its upper edge, then across to the back center of the horn: 3 to 4 inches is average; 4 to 5, good; 5 to 6, excellent; and 6 to 7, ­record book. What protrudes from the horn is only part of the measurement. You need to figure out how many more inches you gain across the horn to its back axis.

The solution: Judge how much prong actually protrudes from the horn and then add about 2 inches for the width of the horn itself. A prong that extends 3 inches is excellent once you add those couple of inches.

Tip: Use the length of the ear, about 6 inches, for ­comparison. If the full prong measurement is almost that long, it’s a keeper.

This is the most critical factor of all, because it’s measured four times after dividing each horn length into quarters. Good mass tends to make horn length and prongs seem shorter than they really are. Circumference is difficult to judge, even for veteran antelope hunters. Complicating things, some animals have partially flattened bases, making them score lower than antelope with more rounded bases. Basal circumference on outstanding bucks will measure 6 to 7 inches.

The Solution: Study videos and books or visit a taxidermy shop to learn the art of ­judging mass. When you’re hunting, wait until the animal turns sideways to check whether the bases are round or flattened before making a circumference estimate.

Tip: Use an antelope’s eye ­circumference for comparison: It’s about 41â„2 inches. Also look at the diameter of the eye, about 11â„2 inches across. If the antelope’s bases look about an inch wider than the eye width, they’ll likely measure over 7 inches—now that’s a pronghorn.

New Climbing Gym in Massachusetts Plans to Give Away All Its Profits

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Berkshire Boulders General Manager Austen McNulty (left) and founder Nick Friedman (right) inside the gym. (Photo: Courtesy Nick Friedman)

The United States has become known for corporate climbing gyms and nationwide chains fraught with labor issues that charge high dollar. A recent analysis by an adventure travel company, which leverages 2026 data as well as the Climbing Business Journal’s 2025 trends report, declared that “the United States is the industry’s laboratory” when it comes to mega-gyms backed by private equity. As climbing becomes more popular, and thus more profitable, one may question whether these gyms will inevitably become the norm.

But in Great Barrington, a small town of a little more than 7,000 residents in Western Massachusetts, entrepreneur Nick Friedman is moving in the opposite direction: starting a new, independent gym that gives away all its profits.

You can trace the 38-year-old’s vision for an altruistic gym back to his childhood, when his dad put his career on hold to demand that General Electric clean up the river that ran through town—and Friedman’s property. In the 1970s, General Electric dumped polychlorinated biphenyls (PCBs) into the Housatonic River, so Friedman’s dad fought to hold the company liable. GE settled, paying $250 million.

In the process, Friedman learned the importance of activism. The cause close to Friedman’s heart is climbing access, which is not exactly easy to come by in Great Barrington or the Berkshires—but not for a lack of rock. While he calls the climbing in Great Barrington world-class, he says that privately owned land and conservation organizations that don’t understand the value of climbing make access a problem.

“Great Barrington is this unique case study in incredible climbing that is barred from true public domain due to land access challenges,” said Friedman, who has been deeply involved in local access work. In 2023, Friedman, who also serves on the board of the Western Massachusetts Climbers’ Coalition (WMCC), purchased a 70-acre parcel in the Farley Ledges climbing area. This bought WMCC time to organize the permits and funding to purchase it from Friedman earlier this year. While Farley Ledges currently remains closed due to an access issue involving a private landowner, WMCC is raising funds to construct a new parking lot and trail to reopen climbing.

Making these land purchases and forging the agreements necessary to maintain and expand access in Western Massachusetts takes a lot of resources, Friedman explains. That’s where his new gym, Berkshire Boulders, which opens on November 6, comes in. He plans to donate 100% of his profits to community organizations, including WMCC and Access Fund.

“The WMCC is very excited about the opening of Berkshire Boulders and the potential it has to positively impact climbing access in Western Massachusetts,” said Regina Madden, WMCC communications coordinator. Protecting and maintaining access requires ongoing resources, so a local business committed to supporting that mission creates an excellent opportunity. In addition, Berkshire Boulders can serve to bridge the gap between indoor and outdoor climbing, helping to foster a stronger sense of stewardship and community.

While Friedman may pursue nonprofit status down the line, he says it was faster to get the gym off the ground as a business that gave away its profits, rather than a 501(c)3 nonprofit. And as someone who has specialized professionally in startups, he adds that he’s always been “intrigued” by hybrid models that blend for-profit business models with nonprofit values.

Currently, the organizations Berkshire Boulders plans to support include WMCC and Access Fund, as well as non-climbing local organizations including the Railroad Street Youth Project and Greenagers, which fosters sustainability work among youth.

“I felt that a climbing gym could play a central role in that effort and become this anchor and the starting point for a broader vision of bringing outdoor recreation more front and center in this small town,” Friedman said.

Friedman doesn’t see accessibility as a binary, “indoors or outdoors” issue; he views it holistically. This is why he’s made it the gym’s mission to improve climbing access indoors and out. He envisions a gym as a place where you can learn the sport, meet partners, and find people to help you make the gym-to-crag transition. In Friedman’s view, that gym-to-crag transition is the end game. That’s why he decorated Berkshire Boulders’ walls with photographs of Great Barrington rock, and rents out crashpads to climbers who want to try bouldering outside, but lack the gear.

But the other end game is community, which he says is hard to foster when you’re answering to private equity investors. Berkshire Boulders’ KPIs will instead revolve “around community impact.”

One way he’s doing that is by offering hardship memberships. Each month, Berkshire Boulders will award free memberships to low-income individuals through a lottery system. This addresses the known problem of affordability in the gym industry—one so notable that the American Alpine Club declared it an access issue in 2024. Friedman says he’s already approved one hardship membership application, but doesn’t have a monthly quota in mind yet. “We do expect to expand that program significantly over the next several months,” Friedman said.

A focus on community KPIs, however, will not come at the expense of a high-quality bouldering facility. Friedman insists that he is committed to a top-tier facility. He is partnering with local hold-maker Decoy and plans to change the setting across the 15-foot-high walls every two weeks. The gym also features a custom-built, “artistically created” adjustable spray wall. Using the Clift app, climbers will be able to set sequences, replicate local outdoor problems, and track leaderboards.

Currently, for Friedman, Berkshire Boulders is a passion project. His personal endgame is not to make a living off the gym—he still works full-time as the chief strategy officer at a small-batch cannabis startup. He also hired an experienced general manager to run the gym. Rather, that Friedman hopes to ultimately do is drive positive change in his local climbing community.

“Personally, I would love to see the Great Barrington climbing become a published resource for the public,” Friedman said. It’s a long game, but a worthy one, in his view, to turn the local climbing from an underground, tricky access dance to a veritable destination with routes documented on Mountain Project, a guidebook, and formal parking and approach trails.

Beyond local impact, could Berkshire Boulders, if successful, inspire a rising tide of small, mission-driven gyms in this era of indoor climbing corporatization? Friedman believes smaller gyms with a focus on community have a better shot at bringing together passionate climbers than the larger conglomerate gyms. “I would love to see an expansion of smaller independent gyms regionally,” he says.

All of Princess Kate’s Remembrance Sunday Looks Over the Years

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The Princess of Wales first attended the event in 2011

Princess Catherine has joined the royal family for the National Service of Remembrance every year since 2011, the same year that she got married to Prince William. The somber annual ceremony, held on Remembrance Sunday at the Cenotaph, a war memorial in London, honors service members who have lost their lives at war. It takes place on the second Sunday of November, and it involves a church service, a wreath-laying ceremony, and a formal procession, all of which are attended by various members of the British royal family.

When it comes to her royal style, the Princess of Wales has long embraced a more sustainable approach, frequently rewearing dresses, jewelry, and accessories. Over the years, that style philosophy has been on full display at Remembrance Sunday, where Kate has made a tradition of recycling looks and adding sentimental touches through meaningful brooches and heirloom jewelry.

One of her signature accessories is the red poppy pin, a symbol that has come to be widely associated with the holiday. Kate is often seen wearing three poppies at once, and while the royal family has never confirmed the significance behind the number, one theory suggests it may be a personal nod to her great-grandmother’s three brothers, all of whom lost their lives during World War I.

Throughout the years, Kate has also worn pieces that once belonged to late royal family members like Queen Elizabeth II and Princess Diana, which she has styled with outfits from Catherine Walker, Alexander McQueen, Temperley London, Diane von Furstenberg, and more.

Ahead, take a look back at Princess Kate’s Remembrance Sunday style through the years, from her first appearance in 2011 to now. And be sure to check back for more updates once the Princess of Wales steps out for the 2026 ceremony, too.

For her first appearance with the royal family at the Cenotaph memorial, Kate wore a black Diane von Furstenberg coat adorned with a double poppy pin along with a Jane Corbett hat and Kiki McDonough earrings.

At the 2012 Remembrance Sunday ceremony, the then-Duchess of Cambridge rewore her Diane von Furstenberg coat from the previous year, this time paired with a Philip Treacy hat, Annoushka earrings, and a single sparkling poppy brooch.

Opting for a different outerwear look for her third appearance, Kate wore a single-breasted Temperley London coat, which she accessorized with a Sylvia Fletcher for Lock & Co. hat and Kiki McDonough hoop earrings with Annoushka pearl drops.

In 2014, Kate wore a black Alexander McQueen jacket over a black ruffled BCBG dress. She rewore the Jane Corbett hat she donned in 2011, and she added a floral brooch, a Mappin & Webb diamond necklace, and Princess Diana’s sapphire earrings.

Kate stepped out in another Alexander McQueen coat for the 2015 Remembrance Day service, which featured velvet trim along the collar, sleeves, and waist. The then-duchess topped it off with a Lock & Co. hat adorned with a sculptural butterfly and black veil, the Kiki McDonough hoop earrings and Annoushka pearl drops she’d worn in 2013, and a triple-poppy pin on her lapel.

In another rewear, Kate donned the same Diane von Furstenberg coat she wore to her first and second Remembrance Sunday services in 2011 and 2012. This time, she styled it with a John Boyd hat, diamond-and-pearl earrings, and three poppies on her lapel.

Kate switched up her look for the 2017 National Service of Remembrance, opting for a double-breasted, collarless Dolce & Gabbana coat with oversized gold buttons, statement Oscar de la Renta earrings in gold, and a Philip Treacy hat she wore in 2006, during her early days of dating Prince William. She also debuted a new hair look, pinning it up to create the illusion of a short bob.

The 2018 Remembrance Day Service marked a significant milestone: the 100th anniversary of the end of World War I. For the occasion, Kate wore a custom Alexander McQueen jacket with military-inspired details, including a high white collar, red shoulder embellishments, and crest-style buttons. She completed the look with a Lock & Co. hat, a triple red poppy pin, and a brooch honoring the women who served during World War I.

Opting for another military-inspired jacket, Kate wore a Catherine Walker coat and a Philip Treacy hat featuring a black veil. Forgoing her usual poppy pin, she instead wore a special pin known as the “Codebreaker’s Brooch” in honor of her grandmother who worked at Bletchley Park during World War II.

At the socially distanced 2020 Remembrance Day ceremony, held amid the COVID-19 pandemic, Kate once again opted for a military-inspired look. She wore a Catherine Walker jacket with fringe detailing at the shoulders, complete with a Philip Treacy fascinator, pearl drop earrings, and her signature triple-poppy pin.

Kate brought back her Alexander McQueen coat from the 2018 service for the 2021 Remembrance Day ceremony. She paired the repeat look with Princess Diana’s pearl earrings, a wide-brimmed Lock & Co. hat, and her signature triple-poppy pin, along with a crystal flower brooch from the Royal British Legion.

For her first Remembrance Day Service following Queen Elizabeth II’s passing, the Princess of Wales wore a black Catherine Walker coat, Princess Diana’s pearl-and-diamond earrings, and a wide-brimmed Philip Treacy hat. She completed the look with her signature trio of poppies, paired with an Art Deco-inspired diamond brooch.

The Princess of Wales opted for multiple rewears at the 2023 Remembrance Sunday service. She repeated the custom Catherine Walker coat she first wore in 2019 and the Philip Treacy hat she previously wore at the 2012 and 2017 services. Then, for some added sparkle, she wore Queen Elizabeth’s diamond-and-pearl earrings, and her signature three-poppy pin.

Kate didn’t make many public appearances in 2024 due to her cancer diagnosis, but she still attended the Remembrance Sunday service that year. She wore a Catherine Walker coat dress, featuring a velvet bow collar, and she accessorized with a Juliette Botterill hat, Queen Elizabeth’s earrings, and her triple-poppy pin.

Kate’s look at the 2025 Remembrance Sunday service featured a rewear with a new twist. She opted for the same Catherine Walker coat from 2022, but this time, she styled it with a lace navy-blue pie-crust collar blouse draping out of the top. She accessorized with a sculptural Lock & Co. fascinator, Princess Diana’s pearl-drop earrings, a single poppy brooch, and a Royal Air Force brooch.

Gnosis Pay breach exposes card details, personal data and crypto wallet information

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The crypto debit card provider said customer funds remained safe despite the exposure of sensitive financial and personal information.

Crypto-linked Visa debit card provider Gnosis Pay disclosed a security breach on Friday that exposed customers’ payment card details, home addresses and cryptocurrency wallet addresses.

Gnosis Pay said it detected unauthorized access to its legacy consumer card system on Nov. 3 and began containing the breach within two hours. The company said customer funds and its separate enterprise platform, which powers white-label card programs, were unaffected.

According to breach notifications reviewed by Cointelegraph, the company identified exposed information including full payment card numbers, expiration dates, CVV codes, customer names, home addresses and wallet addresses linked to affected accounts. The compromised data also included email addresses, phone numbers, nationalities and, in some cases, banking details such as IBAN and BIC information.

Gnosis Pay customer shares a breach notification detailing exposed personal and financial data. Source: Lefteris Karapetsas

One notification also confirmed that the customer’s card was canceled on Nov. 3, with no suspicious transactions or movement of funds detected.

Gnosis Pay said it replaced compromised security credentials, addressed the vulnerability and made 3D Secure verification mandatory for online payments until the legacy system’s planned retirement on Dec. 20. It also warned customers about phishing attempts using their personal or transaction details and said it was arranging monitoring for potential misuse of the stolen data.

According to data from OpenRate, a third-party platform tracking crypto card activity, Gnosis Pay had around 2,400 daily active cardholders and processed $263,200 in transactions over the previous 24 hours.

The company did not disclose how many customers were affected and did not immediately respond to Cointelegraph’s request for comment.

Late Dallmann and Damnjanović goals keep Bayern Munich perfect against Werder Bremen

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

To help deliver timely coverage of women's football, we may use AI tools to support content creation and editing. While we strive for accuracy and editorial quality, readers should independently verify important information.

Bayern Munich beat Werder Bremen 3-1 at the FC Bayern Campus on Friday 6 November, scoring twice in the final seven minutes to make it nine wins from nine in the Frauen Bundesliga. Edna Imade had given José Barcala’s side a first-half lead before Elena Dhont levelled for the visitors, and the game stayed balanced until the 84th minute.

Then Linda Dallmann struck. Three minutes later Jovana Damnjanović added a third from a Momoko Tanikawa pass.

Bayern controlled the ball from the start, finishing with 61% possession and 478 passes to Bremen’s 310. The breakthrough came in the 21st minute, when Imade converted a pass from Mattner-Trembleau. It was Imade’s fourth league goal of the season, putting her level with Bremer, Freigang, Linberg and Stoldt and two behind team-mate Bühl.

The first half grew scrappy. Michelle Weiß was booked for Bremen in the 31st minute and Pernille Harder went into the book for Bayern 5 minutes later. The hosts took a 1-0 lead into the break.

Bremen needed only 5 minutes of the second half to draw level. Lilli Purtscheller set up Dhont in the 50th minute, and for more than half an hour the visitors held firm. Imade and Vanessa Gilles were both cautioned within 3 minutes of each other shortly after the equaliser.

Bayern’s pressure was relentless, though. They finished with 18 shots to Bremen’s 4, 14 of them from inside the box, and forced 4 corners while Bremen won none. Their expected goals total of 2.16 was more than double Bremen’s 1.05. The visitors committed 17 fouls to Bayern’s 10, with Mei Shimada booked in the 80th minute.

Dallmann, who played the full 90 minutes, restored the lead in the 84th minute. Damnjanović, on the pitch for just 18 minutes, made the game safe in the 87th. Michelle Ulbrich’s booking a minute later was the last notable act.

Tanikawa made the difference from the bench. In 27 minutes she created 5 chances, including the assist for the third goal, and was rated 7.7, the highest on the pitch. Imade was close behind on 7.68.

Bayern sit top on 27 points with a goal difference of 24, 3 points clear of VfL Wolfsburg. They are unbeaten in six matches in all competitions, with a 2-2 draw against Real Madrid the only game in that run they did not win.

The head-to-head record keeps getting worse for Bremen. Bayern have now won the last six meetings between the clubs, including a 2-0 win in Bremen on 29 April 2026 and a 4-0 victory in Munich on 4 October 2025.

Defeat ended a three-match winning run for Thomas Horsch’s side, who had beaten SC Freiburg, 1899 Hoffenheim and Mainz 05 in succession. Bremen are fourth on 16 points, one behind Eintracht Frankfurt in third.

Bayern turn to Europe next, travelling to Paris Saint Germain in the UEFA Champions League on Tuesday 10 November. Bremen are back at home on Saturday 14 November, when Union Berlin visit in the Frauen Bundesliga.

Calò’s agent slams Mancini after Italy snub: ‘I hope meritocracy isn’t dead’

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Giacomo Calò’s agent has criticised Roberto Mancini for leaving the Frosinone midfielder out of the Italy squad for the Nations League games against France and Belgium. He warned that the decision risks being “the death of meritocracy”.

According to Vincenzo Pisacane, via TuttoMercatoWeb, who represents the 29-year-old, Calò’s numbers make him the best midfielder in Italy right now, and he can’t understand why the Frosinone playmaker was overlooked.

Mancini named a 31-man squad on Thursday for the games against France at San Siro on 12 November and Belgium in Brussels three days later. He picked ten midfielders, including first call-ups for teenagers Riccardo Cassano of Como and Simone Lontani of Parma, but Calò was not among them.

Calò has been one of the surprises of the season. He spent most of his career in Serie B and Serie C, and in his first top-flight campaign the regista has five league goals, including goals in four games in a row. No player in the division has created more chances. Frosinone, promoted last season thanks largely to his 10 goals and 15 assists, sit sixth in the table, ahead of Juventus and Napoli.

“It was strange not to see him in Roberto Mancini’s squad. I know Mancini, and I know he takes these things into account,” Pisacane told TuttoMercatoWeb. “If he were 34 or 35, I could have understood it, but he’s 29. On numbers, he’s currently the best midfielder in Italy.

“The players Mancini called up are all very strong, but I don’t think any of them have Calò’s numbers at the moment. That’s why it would have been right to call him up. I hope yesterday’s omission isn’t the death of meritocracy and that Giacomo can be called up later if he keeps this up. Otherwise, it means there’s no logic to the national team call-ups.”

Calò’s form could have taken him away from Frosinone in the summer, but Pisacane revealed that both the player and the club rejected an approach from Sampdoria.

“There was very strong interest from Sampdoria at one point, but Calò always wanted to stay, and the club itself stood in the way and tied him down,” he said.

“I can say that a lot of Serie A clubs I had called before have got back in touch to tell me, ‘What a mistake I made not listening to you.’ That’s already important. There will be time to talk about the market and the future, but right now Giacomo is 100% focused on Frosinone.”

Pisacane also credited coach Massimiliano Alvini, who previously worked with Calò at Cosenza and pushed for his signing.

“His relationship with Alvini is wonderful. Both he and director Castagnini really wanted him last year and did everything to sign him, and he’s fully repaying that trust,” he said. “For me, Alvini has achieved far too little compared to what he could have.”

“Giacomo is calm, though, and even more focused and motivated for the game against Cagliari.”

Charlton’s struggles expose ‘absolute ocean’ between WSL and second tier

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Pointless after eight games, Karen Hills’ team are struggling to compete against clubs with chunkier wallets

Played eight, lost eight. Welcome to the Women’s Super League, Charlton Athletic, a club in danger of being chewed up, toyed with and spat straight back out by the top tier.

Bruised by their 6-0 defeat against Chelsea on Sunday, last season’s playoff winners are finding WSL life tough, conceding more than three goals per game on average and struggling to compete against clubs with chunkier wallets. But are they underperforming or are they merely the victims of their own success?

Promoted against the odds last season, defying some of the bigger-spending sides in the second tier such as Newcastle and Bristol City, Karen Hills’ team overachieved by eventually beating Leicester on penalties to secure a top-flight spot. And that was the last time they had anything to cheer about. Sunday’s trip to second-bottom Crystal Palace, another of the promoted sides, theoretically offers Charlton one of their better opportunities to match the 2026 UK Eurovision entry Look Mum No Computer and register one point.

Charlton have been unfortunate. The club-record signing, Linda Líf Boama, suffered a serious knee injury in September, while another of their most important additions, the France defender Hawa Cissoko, signed for her top-tier experience, is with an ankle injury. It would, therefore, be wrong to say there was no serious attempt to strengthen the squad before this season, but Charlton’s fellow promoted teams’ summer business was undeniably more ambitious, whether it be Birmingham’s capture of two Manchester United players or Palace signing the former Tottenham captain Bethany England and the WSL2 Golden Boot winner Lexi Lloyd-Smith.

Charlton lost significant cash in WSL2. The club’s financial accounts for the year ending 30 June 2025 – which were filed in October – stated that the women’s team made a loss of £1.32m, that they had net liabilities of £2.18m and that additional funding would be required. Precise details on wage bills, revenue and expenditure were not published after the company was deemed to be entitled to exemption from audit under section 479A of the Companies Act 2006.

Promotion to the WSL also comes with additional expenditure needed to meet enhanced minimum requirements and, not insignificantly, higher minimum salaries. In WSL2 last season, clubs had to pay players aged 23 and over a minimum salary of £26,900; aged 21 and 22 a minimum of £22,200; and those aged 18 to 20 at least £17,500. In the top tier, those minimum salaries rise to £42,500, £34,700 and £26,900 respectively.

Meanwhile, central income is modest; WSL Football’s financial accounts for 2024-25 stated that £9.41m was paid to their 24 member clubs, approximately £400,000 each. Unlike promotion to the men’s top tier, which can transform a club’s financial fortunes, the main benefit of reaching the WSL for a women’s team is the potential for greater exposure on Sky Sports and the BBC’s broadcast coverage. Charlton, it should be noted, also do not have the comfort blanket of a wealthy men’s team owner to invest in their women’s side.

There is a feeling within the women’s game that summer recruitment was somewhat rushed. Their first signing was not announced until 18 July. In contrast, Birmingham’s head coach, Amy Merricks, spoke repeatedly of trying to become “WSL ready” as early as the 2024-25 campaign.

Kick off your evenings with the Guardian's take on the world of football

Birmingham and Palace have both drawn at Arsenal this season and caused other sides problems, whereas Charlton – only the fifth team to lose their opening eight WSL games – have struggled to keep the ball, completing only six sequences of 10 or more passes, averaging only a 65% pass accuracy. All three teams, however, are finding the step up to the top tier a challenge and it is not surprising that the WSL’s bottom three are the promoted trio. Merricks, whose team travel to London City Lionesses on Sunday, said on Friday that “the way the table looks now is probably a way off how it will finish” but the gap between the divisions was “an absolute ocean”.

Asked how wide she feels the gulf is, Hills, who says her Charlton players have “prepared really well this week”, replied: “We’re trying to make the jump and trying to stay with this pack. The gap is continuing to get bigger and you see with some of the top teams the players that they’ve brought in and the finances and the resources that they have. It’s going to be a big challenge and we’re finding that out on a weekly basis.”

How to be unemployed: Glenn Kelman on life after Redfin

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

by Glenn Kelman on Nov 6, 2026 at 12:47 pmNovember 6, 2026 at 12:52 pm

After 20 years at Redfin, and 10 months of unemployment, I’ve co-founded a new company. My business partner and I will announce our plans after we’ve built our product, but in the meantime GeekWire asked me to explain what I learned in the transition between Redfin and this new venture.

Many of us are in transition because society is in transition, with AI hollowing out big companies and making it easier to start new companies.

In the past, my own transitions had been harrowing, because I ran out of money or lost my sense of purpose. Leaving Redfin, I thought “here we go again.” I felt self-conscious being in the grocery store at 11 a.m. on a weekday. I was ashamed when I couldn’t come up with an idea for a company. But I made it to the other side.

To do that, I had to convince myself that I was an intrepid explorer, even if what I was exploring wasn’t Mount Everest, but instead what I could be. I wanted to feel brave about encounters I otherwise would’ve found embarrassing. Over the last year, I met people about becoming a high school teacher, a Whistler bagel shopkeeper, a candidate for the state legislature, the founder of a tree-planting nonprofit, the CEO of a pre-IPO company and a project leader for Sound Transit.

The way to prepare yourself to do one thing for a long time is first to consider everything else.

At the beginning of my exploration, a CEO recruiter told me the startup scene in Seattle was “dead” and that, for all of Seattle’s talent, more companies were forming in New York and even LA. I Googled “high-growth Seattle startups” and the list included one company that went bankrupt in 2023, and Redfin, which was acquired in 2025.

Another friend told me that she was on a Discord channel with a bunch of CTOs who were miserable trying to do “AI transformations” at businesses with legacy codebases. At least for me, the logical response to all this has been to start an AI-native company in Seattle.

“The way to prepare yourself to do one thing for a long time is first to consider everything else.”

It took me months to feel ready to do that. In January, a friend told me I wasn’t allowed to make any big decisions until September. Another said I had to wait until the frenzy of my last job had subsided. “You’ll know it has happened,” he said, “because food will start tasting better” (it never happened).

At a conference, a fashion entrepreneur who’d built and abandoned vast residences in Jackson Hole and Costa Rica, and got divorced twice in the past three years, gave me a bracelet that said “we are enough.”

Since I’d often been thinking I hadn’t done enough in the morning to deserve lunch, it was a relief to feel that I didn’t have to be creative or productive in order to justify my existence. But relieving that pressure also made it easier to be creative and productive.

On a call walking through Volunteer Park, I told a friend that the bracelet reminded me I could be happy and never start a company again. She said, “YOU JUST NEED A CO-FOUNDER.”

She told me to share flawed ideas with people who could make them better. I set up meetings with potential co-founders, and we shared our ideas before they were ready, because our meetings had become deadlines. Then we used AI to turn our ideas into products, so we could see what was good and bad about each idea. Starting a company started to feel like a natural progression, instead of Athena sprouting out of Zeus’s head.

When I confessed to a friend that everything we were working on still seemed so small, she said, “What about breakfast burritos?” She outlined her vision for a shop serving breakfast burritos all day, with a line out the door. Then she started talking about inventing a nursing home “that makes everyone happy.” Rather than running a McKinsey market-sizing analysis, she just thought about what she wanted to exist in the world.

That’s something almost anyone can do. In her living room, I suddenly felt that humanity would always have an instinct — this is a bizarre word, but it’s what popped into my head — to fructify. I remember walking home from her house with my wife and hearing the thick sound of insects humming in the night as proof of life’s rich pageant. This is what I mean by surrounding yourself with good vibes.

The last step in my process maybe should’ve come first, choosing where I wanted to make a difference. I thought about all the companies that I honestly would’ve been proud to start, and they were all in the same industry. I had no experience in that industry, but a friend said I could offset that if I were willing to give it everything I have, which is what I’m now doing. I met amazing, generous investors who gave me a good idea, and a business partner who helped me think it through.

Through all of this, I depended on my wife more than any husband ever should. She works herself to the bone treating people with cancer, but she took my calls in the middle of her workday, when I would talk in detail about what to cook for dinner and then — one more thing! — what’s the meaning of my life?

Once, she was getting into our Chevy Bolt for her morning commute while I was rounding the corner with the dog, and I saw her brighten in the way many people did back when I was Redfin’s CEO.

I found myself full of anticipation. She reached into the car, as if to grab a small gift, and heaved something at me from across the street, saying it “needs reprogramming.” Before I realized it was a garage clicker, I thought she had said I was the one who needed reprogramming. “Maybe that’s true,” I said to myself, trudging past the browned lawn, and the dirty dishes, and into a world of possibilities.

In just a few weeks, AWS re:Invent brings advanced and expert-level sessions on the services you build on every day, from serverless and containers to databases and developer tools. Go hands-on with new launches the same week they’re announced, and hear directly from the engineers who built them. Experience the depth you won’t find anywhere else.

Josh O’Connor Should Be James Bond

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The Challengers star would bring a new energy—and a new brand of modern masculinity—to the world’s most famous spy.

WHEN THE NAMES of the rumored final contenders to play the next James Bond were floated in the press a couple of weeks ago—seemingly Callum Turner, Jack Lowden, Josh O’Connor, and a mystery third candidate, according to Deadline—I heard a lot of the same sentiment afterward. In more or less words, a lot of people seemed to say “I love Josh O’Connor, but he shouldn’t be Bond.” When I dropped the link into a group chat full of people who aren’t inhaling movie news like I am, there was another probably common response: “I don’t know who that guy is, but he doesn’t look like Bond.”

To me, that sentiment is exactly why O’Connor is the best—and, in some ways, the only—choice to be the next James Bond.

For those not familiar, O’Connor, a 36-year-old Brit who grew up in Cheltenham, has been a working actor for more than a decade, but his star has only really begun to shine in the 2020s. He won an Emmy for playing Prince Charles in The Crown, and more recently has made splashes in movies like the thrilling and sexy sports film Challengers, Rian Johnson’s third Knives Out film Wake Up Dead Man (which, ironically, he appeared in alongside previous Bond Daniel Craig), and this summer’s Steven Spielberg blockbuster Disclosure Day.

It’s not hard to understand why some people’s minds might naturally push against the idea of O’Connor as Bond. Where 007 is typically a classically handsome, devilishly smooth gentleman who almost looks like a video game simulation—seriously, look back at Sean Connery and Pierce Brosnan for just a second—O’Connor is a bit off-kilter. His hair is wavy; his ears are prominent; his smile is asymmetrical. There’s no beating around the bush—he’s been compared more often to Linguini from Pixar’s Ratatouille than the idea most people have of what a James Bond should be.

But it’s through those eccentricities that O’Connor shines. If you take the time to watch his performances, you’ll find the constant image of someone who brings an undeniable charisma to every project, and somehow manages to mold that into something different each time. In Challengers, O’Connor is the kind of guy you want to root against—he’s sleazy, not a particularly good friend, and unrefined. But he brings the perfect bit of steamy, compelling energy to a love triangle that wouldn’t be the same without him. Co-stars Mike Faist and Zendaya are deserving of credit (as are composers Trent Reznor and Atticus Ross), but O’Connor’s charm is what makes the whole movie work.

He pulls a similar magic trick with just about every other performance. In Wake Up Dead Man, he plays a former boxer-turned-priest who once accidentally killed a man in the ring. Throughout the course of the film, O’Connor goes from someone who wrestles with guilt, shame, and intrigue to someone who can believably talk someone struggling with grief off a ledge—all while both trying to solve a murder and possibly being suspected of that same murder at the same time. In Kelly Reichardt’s The Mastermind, O’Connor plays a less-than-competent, hardly aspirational art thief who stumbles his way through charged political times. Despite this, O’Connor is so magnetic on screen that it’s just about impossible not to like him.

But it’s not just the way he elevates sleazeballs and losers into undeniably watchable men (although that doesn’t hurt). O’Connor has shown a propensity to play traditionally enchanting as well. In La Chimera, from Italian filmmaker Alice Rohrwacher, he’s cool, sleek, and morally gray in a movie that may be the blueprint for what his Bond could potentially look like. He’s got a likable way about him that’s different from some of the manly men (like Connery), sleek operators (like Brosnan), or rugged action stars (like Craig) who’ve played the role before; His charm is distinctly more modern, oozing from nothing other than being a guy who knows how to talk to people, and do it really well.

O’Connor’s quirks and oddities, may, at first glance, be what seems to make him an unfit choice to play Bond. But the key is to look at O’Connor’s unique look and gifts as a feature rather than a bug. People might say Bond needs to look this way, and sound that way, and otherwise it doesn’t work. But for anyone who feels so strongly about that, there’s good news: There are 25 other canonical James Bond movies (plus 1967’s Casino Royale and 1983’s Never Say Never Again) where he almost always looks and behaves just like that. When better than now to try something new? Daniel Craig’s blond hair and untraditional look were also questioned at first, and it’s safe to say that risk turned out pretty well in the end.

The forthcoming Bond movie has Denis Villeneuve signed on as director, and Villeneuve has pretty much never failed when it comes to casting his movies. Can you imagine anyone other than Timothée Chalamet as Paul Atreides in his Dune movies? It seems like an obvious pick now, but the 30-year-old Oscar nominee had only just begun to break out when he was cast in the role back in 2018. Villeneuve has a clear eye for talent, and so it won’t be much of a stretch to trust in whoever ultimately lands the job—but it’s hard to not be excited about a director of his caliber linking up with an actor as ambitious and high-reaching as O’Connor.

Plus, it’s worth noting that the Bond franchise isn’t quite like other mega-series out there. Since the Craig era began with 2006’s Casino Royale, Bond movies have come out on average every three or four years. That’s spread out enough for O’Connor to continue taking on fun, exciting projects even outside of the role as everyone’s favorite super spy.

The other choices aren’t bad. Callum Turner gets a bad rap after his appearance in One Night Only, but he’s shown promise in projects like Green Room and Masters of the Air, and Apple TV’s new Neuromancer looks good. Jack Lowden has played one of television’s most beloved spies for six seasons on Slow Horses—the fit would be seamless. Whoever Villeneuve picks (even if it’s the unnamed mystery actor) will be afforded a huge amount of goodwill.

But isn’t the fact that O’Connor elicits such a reaction—consistently in his on-screen roles and through the mere idea of him becoming James Bond—proof that he’s the right pick?
